Unlike t~e previous Cenauses, when the SubsIdiary Tables were printed alongwith the Main
Tables, this volume has been exclusively devoted
to the Subsidiary Tables of the Main Tables. The
only exception are the Subsidiary Tables to the
Housing Tables (E-Series Tables), which have been
pUblished in Part IV-Report on Housing and
Establishments.
The Subsidiary Tables are based on the data
contained in the Main Tables published in Parts
II-A, II-B, III and II-C of Vol. XX, 1961 Census,
Himachal Pradesh. These Tables lJring out the
salient features of the Main Tables by displaying
proportionate figures, in the various forms, for the
administrative units of the Union Territory of
Himachal Pradesh.
The purpose of the Subsidiary Tables is to make
it easier to comprehend the various characteristics
of the Main Tables. Thus a Subsidiary Table dearly
brings to light the substance of a Main Table and
at the same time makes it possible to do away
with the bulky absolute figures given in the Main
Table. In this sense the Subsidiary Tables surpass
even the Main Tables in their utility in certain
cases.

An introductory note explaining the various
concepts connected with the data collected in 1961
Census precedes these tables. Further, all the Substdiary Tables are preceded by a fly-leaf. explaining
the source, the contents and the method of its preparation.
My thanks ~re due to the Manager and staff of
the Government of India Press, for their whole
hearted cooperation and help for bringing out
this publication.

BosweU,

INTRODUCTORY NOTE
The Subsidiary Tables

In all 70 Subsidiary Tables have been prepared
and printer in this part of the 1961 Oensus Report
in respect 'pf Himachal Pradesh. These tables are
4J~sed on A-Series-General Population Tables, BSeries-Economic and Household Economic Tables
C-Series and'D-Series-Socill.l, Cultural a:nd Migra~
tion Tables, or the Main Tables. All the Main
Tables except \Household EconomIc Tables, have
been prepared on full count. The Household Economic Tables are '\lased on 20% Sample Housenolds.
Subsidiary Tables, based on E-Series- Housing
Tables, have beel;l printed in Part IV-Report '(;m
Housing and Est{tblishments, of the 1961 Census
Report in respect M Himachal Pradesh, whereas no
Subsidiary Tablesthave been prepared in respect
of Special Tables for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes SCT, SC an.d ST-Series-Main Tables.
The material on which these Subsidiary Tables are
based has been printed as indicated hereunder:-

Scheme of Presentation
A series-wise presentation of the Tables has
boon made in this volume. All the series appear one
~er the other ~lphabetically. This is intended to
~lve homogeneltr and comparability to the

Subsidiary Tables. All the Subsidiary 'rabIes have
been prepared for the Union Territory and its six
districts of Chamba, Mandi, Bilaspur, Mahasll,
Sirmur and Kinnaur except a few cases where the
lVlain Tables related to the urban areas only or where
the district-wise data is not a.vailable.
A fly leafbearingi>rief description of the material
on which a particular Subsidiary Table is based and
the method followed for its compilation, appears
before every Subsidiary Table. It is intended to
facilitate better a.nd clear comprehension of thes~
Subsidiary Tables.

The 1961 Census
The Ma.in Tables on wh:ich these Subsidiary
Tables are based were prepared on the basis of
statistics collected by enumerators, who visited
every household in their respective blocks during
specific period 10th February to 1st March 1961
excepting certain snowbound areas where an ~arlier
count was made, and canvassed on individual slip
for every person and a household schedule for every
household. A revisional round of three days was
conducted to make relevant changes in the record
due to the births and deaths that might have
occurred before the 1st March 1961 and after the
actual enumeration. The changes made relate to
only numbers and not to any other data e.g. changes occurring in economic activity of cert2in persons, or age of certain persons etc. The data recorded in these forms was tabulated so we have the
above mentioned, A-Series, B-Series, C and DSeries, E-Seri~s, SO-Series, SOT--Series and STSeries Ta.bles. The individual slip, the houseMld
schedule, a.nd t~e Cknsus Population record (which
was actually prmted on the backside of the household schedule) are being reproduced here as AnnexUTe I.to this int.roductory note.

Rural and Urban Classification
To qualify for an urban area, a place should
first be either a Municipal Committee or a Municipal Area, or under a Small Town Committee or a
Notified Area Committee or a Cantonment Board.
Consequently all localities which had local bodies
in 1961-there were 12 such localities or had a local
body in 1951 and was also counted a~ a town then
-there was only one sllc}llocality were iI).dud.ed

2
in urban areas in the Census of 1961. The number of
urban areas increased from 11 in 1951 to 13 in 1961
by the formation of two new towns of Narkanda and
Shri Naina Devi Ji in the light of the above definition. The remaining parts of Himachal Pradesh were
treated as rural areas. Table A-IV printed in Part
II-A in respect of Him:cchal PrDdesh rd "tJS
exclmively to town'!.
It may be of interest to explain here the variou s
concepts connected with the data collected in 1961
Census.

Workers and Non-Workers
In 1961 Census population, for economic classification has been divided into (i) Workers and (ii)
Non-WorkErs. In the previoui; Censuses economic
classification was don€. in terms of categories like
economically independent, "Semi-independent"
"Earning dependent" or "Non-earning dependf'ut"
i.e. purely on the basis of 'in{'ome', if any. In these
Censuses the term 'actual worker' included even
persons who were receiving ',income without doing
work such as rentier~, royalty earners, retirees etc.
These concepts of 'dependency' and 'income' gave
wav to the concept of actual 'work' in 1961 Census.
Ma"'ny women who would otherwise be dependent
have found place among workers on the basis of
actual agricultural work or work copnected with
household industry done by them. Similarly rentires royalty earners, retired persons, begg; ,rs,
who although earn thcir livelihood in vne way or
the other have been classified as non-workers in
1£61 Census.
So far economic classification in 1961 Census is
concerned emphasis was laid on work. All those
who worked, even if a worker derived no income for
himself, such as an apprentice wit.hout stipend or
wages, were workers. Exception wa::> made for an
adult woman who was engaged in household
duties but was doing no other productive work to
augment the famil}r resources. Even part-time
workers, such as women and children, who were engaged in work, such as rice pounding for sale or wages, or in domestic services for wages or for others
or ~inding cattle or selling firewDod or making and
sellmg cowdung cakes or grass etc. or any such
work were treated as wo.rkers. Work included not;
only actual work but effective supervision and
direction of work. The basis of work would be satisfied in the case of seasonal work like, cultivation,
livestock, dairying, household industry etc., if the
person had had some regular work of more than one
)1o'\lr n da.y throu~holft the ~reater :rart of the

working season. In the case of regular employment
in any trade, profession, service, business or commerce the basis of work would be satisfied if the
person was employed during any of the fifteen
days preceding the day on which the enumeration
took place. A per30n who was working but was
absent from his work during the fifteen days preceding the day on which enumerated or even exceeding the period of fifteen d[1Ys due to illness or other
cause was treated as a worker. Though a person was
deriving an income, but doing nO productive work,
such ~ retired person receiving a pension and..-Jot
employed again, or a rentier or a person living on
agricultural or n()n-agricultural rnyality, rent or
dividend, for securing which he did not have to
work and who did no other work, was a non-worker.
All the working persons engaged in any branch
of productive work are primarily termed as 'Workers' and all others not engaged in any'productive
work are trei1ted as 'Non-workers' in 1961 Census
Economic Classific.ation. The workers have further
been classified into nine broad categorie~, while
non-workers are put in category X-Non-Workers,
which are explained hereunder--

CategoryI-As Cultivator: This category includes
cultivators of land and cultivation includes ploughing' sowing and harvesting. A persen was considered working as a cultivator if engaged as employer,
single worker, or family worker in (a) cultivation of
land or supervision or directiou of cultivation of
land, owned or held from Government and (b)
cultivation of land or supervision or direction of
cultivation of land held from private persons or
Institutions for payment in money, kind or share.
An agricultural labourer wa~, however, not considered as a cultivator. A person engaged In fruit
growing or having orchard or plantations like tea,
was also not a cultivator. Another class of persons
not considered as cultivators was those who had
given out their lands to others for cultivation for
money, kind, or share of crop and who did not even
supervise or direct cultivation of land.
Category II-As Agricultural Labourer: This
cat~goryin.cludes all th~se persons who worked
in another person's land only as labourers without
excercising any supervision or direction·in
cultivation, for wages in cash, kind or ,share
such as share of produce.
Category III-In Mining, Quarrying, Livestock
Forestry, Fishing, Hunting and Plantations,
Orchards and Allied Activities : This category
includes the following cl~sses gf wor~ers :rrovid~q.

I

3
they do not satisfy the condjtions laid down- in
the concept of household industry:(i) Persons working in. mining and quarrying,
(ii) Persons engaged in livestock, breediItg

and hunting,
(iii) Persons engaged in forestry and fishing,
(iv.) Persons engaged in raising plantation
crops, fruits and allied activities.
\

\ . I V-At Household Industry: A houseOategory
hold industry related to prQduction, processing,
servicing, \repairing or makfug and selling,of
goods. But a household industry was not to be
on the scale bf a registered factory. It should have
. been conduCted by the head of the household
himself or *ainly members of the household
at home or \within the village in the rural
areas and oply at home in uroon areas.
Main criterian\ for a household industry was the
participation o\f ohe 6r more members of a household in rural ar~as. In the urban areas the industry
should have been confined to the house. The
concept of household industry as defined for
Census purposes, does not include professions
such as pleader; doctor or barber or washerman
or astrologer. Persons working at household
industry, as defined above, including those working
as paid employees ,in other's household industry,
are classified in this category of workers.

Oategory V-In Manufacturing other than Household Industry: This category of workers includes
all those persons engaged in processing, manufacturing and repairing or making and selling of
goods, p.otjucluded in category IV-At Household
Industry. Persons working in registered factories
are also included in this category.
Oategory VI-In Oonstruction : All persons
engaged in construction and maintenance of
buildings, including erection, flooring, decorative
construction, electrical and sanitary installations
are included in this category. This includes
persons engaged in construction and maintenances
of roads, railways, bridges, tunnels, telegraph
and telephone lin.es, water ways and water reservoirs such as bund, embankments, dam, canal,
tank, tubewells and wells e~c.
Oategory VII-In Trade anil OommeJrce .Persons
engaged or employed in wholesale and retail trade
are included in this category. It also includes
persons employ-ed or engaged in other commercial

activities like importing and exporting of goods
and commodities, stocks, shares, insnrance, money
lending, banking and similar types of financial
operations, auctioneering, distribution of motion
pictures, and all other activities connected with
trade and commerce, including hiring out of durable goods like electric fans, microphones etc.
Oategory VIII-In Transport, Storage and
Oommunieations : This category of workers includes
all the persons engaged or employed in any form
or mode of transport, storage and warehousing
activities. It further includes those engaged in
communications such as postal, telegraphi y, wireless, signal~ telephone, information and broadcasting.
Oategory IX-In Other Servir:es : Persons employed in. electricity, gas, water and sanitary
services, public services, educational and scientific
services, medical and health services, religious
>and welfare services, legal services, business
services, community services and trade and labour
association, recreation services, personal services,
services not classified in these lines and activities
not adequately described are included in this
category.
,
Oategory X-Nan-Workers: This category of
persons includes all those who are not engaged
in any productive work. (i) full time students
(ii) persons attending exclusively to household
duties (iii) dependents, infants and disabled
(iv) retired, rentier or persons of independent
means (v) beggar, vagarants etc. and (vi) inmates
of penal menta'!. and charitable institutions, all
belong to the category of Non-Workers. Thus,
actual productive work, and not the income,
was the distributing factor for workers and nonworkers.
Industrial Classification
In order to make a still deeper study of the
workers (other than Cultivators and Agricultural
Labourers)" further classification of workers rela.ting
to the industrial categories III to IX has been
made on the lines of the Indian Standard Industrial
classification.
The Subsidiary involved are
B-IV.9~ B-XIV A '1, B-XIV B·1, B-XVI ·1, B-XVr.
2 and B-XVI ·3. It is important that the
concept of 'industry' should not be con,fused with
that of 'occupation'.
Industry means that
sector of economic activity in which the earner is
engaged e.g., textile industry, automobile industry
etc., whereas the 'occupation' describes the exact
function that an individual performs in that
~~ctor, e.g., weaver, fitter, carpenter etc.

4
Code structure of Industrial Classification
The following system of notation has been
adopted in the classification of Industries:
DivisiO'I'/,: The entire range of BCon(lmic activ-jty
is divided into nine 'Divisions' and a 'Code number'
is. given to each. The first digit of any code
number represents tlw Division lU1der which
that activity is classified, c.g., the numher given
to the lIIanufacturir.g Division and manllfact1l1'ing
activity of any 16nd will have code numbers.
beginning with 'g' or '3'.

Maim Groups: Each 'Division' is then divided
into a number of 'Major Groups' not exceeding
ten.
The iirst two digits of any code number
together represent the 'Division' and the 'Major
Group' under which that activity is classified.
Thus the number "20" has becn given to tlte
"production of Foodstuffs' (Major Group) under
'Manufacturing" (Division).

Minor GToups: Eri,e,h '1iajor Group' is again
sub-divided into a number of 'Minor Groups'
HDt exceeding ten.
The 'Minor Group' code number, e.g., '5' nndllr M.ajor Group 20 rela.tes to
production of bread, biscuits, cake, etc. Thus, if
we find code number 205 we know that, it means
production. of bread, biscuits, ca.kes etc., (Min.or
Group) in :the 'Foodstuffs Industry' (Major Group)
in "Mallufa.cturing" (Division). Again if we fin.d
uumber 701 we know that it means "Transporting
by tramway, and bus service" (Minor Group")
in the "Transport Industry" (Major Group) in
the "Transport, Storage and Communication"

Occupational Classification
In 1961 Census workers have been classified
according to the National Classification of occupations. Occupation signifies the exact frnlCtion that
a worker performs in line industrial sector,-e.g\
fitter, c'arpenter etc. in any industry e.g. textile'
industry, chemicals industry, beverages industry etc. Subsidiary Tables B-V.1 and B-VI· 1
display data with regard to occupations.
"

National C~ssification of Occupations

I

The code l3tructure of the National Classification
of occupatidp.s is similar to that adopted for Industrial Clas~iiication in as much as this classification is also.\ a three digit classification.
I

Division-The entire range of occupations is
divided into ~O divisions and a code number is
given to each. ~~ The fi,r~t digit of the code number
represents the qivision under which that occupation
is classified e.g. the code number given to the
'Sales workers' is '3' and. sales occupations of any
kind will have code numbers beginning with '3'.
The code number of last Division is X while there
is a composite ,division 7-8.
Grottp-Each Division is then divided into a number of group" not exceeding eleven. The first two
digits of any occupational code number together
represent the Division and the Group under which
that occupation is classified. Thus occupational
group code number '33' represents salesmen, shop
assistants and related workers, under Division '3'sales workers. In case of eleventh Group X is
added to the Division code number.
Family-Each Group is again sub-divided into
a number of occupational families not exceeding
ten. The family is represented by the third digit
added to the group occupational code number
e.g. occupational family code number 330 represents
salesmen and shop assistants wholesale and retail
trade under Group '33' -salesmen, shop assistants and related workers, and under the occupational Division '3' sales workers.
The National Classification of occupations is
reproduced as Annexure III to this note.

Employer, Employee, Single Worker and Family
Worker

(i).An "Employer" is a person who has to employ
other persons to carryon his work. But a person who
. employs domestic servants for household duties
or has subordinates under him in an office where

he is employed by others, is not all employer eveil
if he has power to employ another person on behalf
of his employer or employers.

(ii) .An "Employee" is a person who usually
works for some other person for salary or wages in
cash or kind.
(iii) A "Single Worker" is a person who works by
himself but not as Head of household industry.
He neither -em.ploys anyone nor is employed by
any other person.
(iv) .A "Family W orker"is one of the members of
a household who ordinarily does at least an
hour's work every day dur'ing the working season in
an industry, business or trade conducted by the
household.
~Household

The 1961 Census definition of a 'household' is a
group of persoil" who commonly live together and
would take their meals from a common kitchen
unless the exigencies of work prevented any 0 f
them from doing so. Thus all the persons including
guests, servants, taking meals from the common
kitchen are included in 'household'. Unrelated
persons living together in institutions like Hotels,
Hostels, Sarais, Boarding houses were also treated
as households.

Households engaged in cultivation
A household whose head or some other members
are engaged in cultivation or in active supervision
of cultivation of land is considered to be a cultivating household.

Interests in land
The cultivating households have been further
classified into three classes according to iitterests in
l~nd:-

(i) Households engaged in cultivation of land
entirely owned or held from Government

(ii) Households engaged in cultivation of land
entirely held from private persons or
institutions for payment in money, kind
or share.

(iii) Households engaged in cultivation 0 f
land partly owned or held from Government and partly held from private person s
or institutions for payment in money,
kind or share.

Literacy and Educational Level

3. Primary or Junior Basic

In 1961 Census, a person who could neither
read nor write or could merely read but could not
write in any language was cOllsider(ld illiterate.
A persoll who could both read and write "a
siIuple letter" with understanding was considered
literate.

4. Matriculation or Higher Secon.dary

The first educational level is 'literate without
educational level'. Obviously persons who could
simply read and write without having attained.
even primary standard were included in this category. The next educational level "Primary or
Junior Basic" includes all those who had passed
Primary or Junior Basic but had not attained the
qualifications of a Matriculate. In rural areas all
the person having qualifications of Matriculation
and apove were grouped together in the third
category of "Matriculation and above" whereas
in urban areas. further classification was made as
giyen hereunder :
I

2. Literate (withdut educational level)

NOTE :-1. The rural and urban break· up according 00 the first set of figures takes into account the new definition of towm.
of 1961 Census in addition 00 the territorial changes in the district; while the second set shows only the territorial
changes in the district.
2. As the figures for first set and second set are similar in respect of the districts other than Mahasu District and Union
Territory, only one 8e$ of figures is, therefore, given for those districts.
3. The concopt Il.nd method for working out the first set of figures are as below:.;
"ltrom the total urban figures in the past censuses, the figures of towns which have bene deleted in 1961 Census,
are deducted· This gives comparable urban figures of a district in the past censuses.
Accordingly, 'Kasumpti is treated as a. deleted toWll of Mahasu District in the previous censuses, because it is
not a town in 1961 Census, there are, therefore, two sets of figures in the rural and urban break.up for Mahasu
District and as a result thereof for Himachal Pradesh Union Territory. In the first set of figures, the urban figures
of Kasumpti town for the Census years 1941, 1931 and 1921, when it was a town, are not shown as such but
stand included in rural ligures. In the second set of ngures'the urban figures of Kasumpti town for the said
Census years are shown as suoh, '.' .• included in urban figures.
4. There was a terrioorial change in Mahasu District duringthe last decade when Kinnaur District was carved out
ofit. Theresu1tant figures as an effect of this change, adjusted for the 1951 Census and estimated for the Census
years 1941, 1931 and 1921 have been shown agaimt Kinnaur District.

NOTE : - 1.
'l'llC rural and urban break.up according to the first set offigurcs takes into account the new ·de5.nitio"l of towns
of 1961 Census in addition to the teITitorial changes in the district while the second set shows 6nly the territorial
changes in the distt-ict.
.
2. As the figures for first set and second set are similar in I espeet of the dist( cts other than lIIahasu District and Union
Territory, only one set of figures is, therefore, given 'for those districts
3. The concept and method for working out the first set of figures are as below : "From the total urban figures in the past censuses, the figures of towns 'which have been deleted in 1961 Census,
are deducted. This gives comparable urban figures of a district in the past cenEU~.
Accordingly 'Kasumpti' is treated as a deleted town of Mahasu District in the previous censuses, because it is
not a town in 1961 Census, there are, therefore, two sets of figures in the rural and urban break-up for Mahasu
District and as a result thereof for Himachal Pradesh. Union Territory. In the first set of figures, the urban
figures of Kasumpti town for the Census years 19-H, 1!J31 and 1921, wnen it was a. town, are not shown as Buch
but stand included in rural figures. In the second set of figures the urban figures of Kasumpti town for the
said Census years are shown as such, included in uruan figures.
4. There was a territorial change in lIIahasu District during the last de\lade when Kinnaur District was carved out
of it. The resultant figures as an effect of this change, adjusted for the 1951 Census an'l estimated for the Census years 1941, 1931 and 1921 have been shown against Kinnaur District~

This is the fifth and the last Subsidiary Table
(8. T.) to the Main Table (M. T.) ',A-I Area, Houses
and Population' printed in the Part II-A of the
Himachal pradesh 1961 Census Report.
The varia\ions, i.e., the percentage increase (+ )
decrease (-)\ in the Subsidiary 'fable have been
worked out on the basis of the population- shown
in tables mentJoned 'l;>elow:

It will be seen that the variation figures in
columns 2, 3, 4 and 5 for 'total' in the Subsidiary
'fable are the same as given in the Main Table
A-II. 'Variation in Population During Sixty
Years' printed in the Part II-A of 1961 Census
Report, except that they have been rounded off
to one place of decnual.

In the rural and urban break-up of variations
there are two sets of figllres as explained in the fooL
note to the Subsidiary Table.
The density figures given in Col. 6 of the Subsidiary Table are the same as given in Col. 4 of the
Main Tabl A-I referred above. For the purpose
of working out the 1951 totalfruralJmban
d nsity of the districts in Col. 7 of the Subsidiary
Table for comparative study with the 1961 density
the population in 1951, adjusted to the jurisdiction
of 1961 has been taken into account. The 1961
total area of the respective district has thus been
taken into account as ·'total' area of the district
for 1951. The 1951 'rural' area has been worked out
by substracting the 1951 area of the continuing
towns from the total area of the respective district
for first set of figures and by substracting the 1951
area of the continuing towns and the 1951 area of
- the deleted towns from the total area of the respective district for second set of figures. No
town of 1951 Census has, however, be n e'el t~d in
1961 Census. Abs)lu~e fi:;ure, of area, worked out
a'l above, are given in a statement printed in the
fly-leaf to the Subsidiary Table A-I.3.
Figures for Cols. 6 and 7 have been rounded off
to integers.
Density=Population= Area.
The district of Kinnaur is entirely rural and
hence no urban information for this ·-district.

Absolute figures of PoPull;lotion for the years
1921 to 1951 may be seell for reference in the
Statement 2 printed in the fly-leaf to the Subsidiary
Table A-I·2.

(i) The total of all the Divisions is equal to
10,000.
(ii) The total of all the Major Grot;1pS- in a

Division.
(iii) The total of all the Minor Groups in a

cases, because the Minor Groups having
figures less than. ~ per cent of the respective Divisions (0·5 per cent in case of
Divisions 2 and 3) have been excluded
from the Main TaMe.

The Subsidiary Table gives per 10,000 distribution of workers\ engaged in, activities other than
cultivation and agncultural,labour by Divisions~,
Major Groups and. Minor Groups of the Indian
Standard Industri~l Classification (I.S.I.C.) for
the Union TerritorYl and each of its districts.

the 'Total', i.e. rAll Divisions' figures has been
taken as 10,000. First they have been distributed
in I.S.I.C. Divisions 0 to 9 an,d then further distributed in Major Groups and Minor Groups.

The district of Kinnaur is entirely rural and
, henre nil urban information for the District.
The Indian Standard Industrial Classification
(1.S.1.C.) Divisions, Major Groups and Minor
Groups are indicated by these Code Numbers in
the Subisidiary Table. For study of their concepts
the Code structure of the Standard Industrial
Classification may be seen in the introduction
portion of this publication, which is printed there
as 'Appendix II' to Introduction.

SUBSIDIARY TABLE B-V'l

DISTRIBUTION OF 10,000 WORKERS OTHER THAN THOSE WO_RKING AS CULTIVATORS OR AS AGRICULTURAL LABOU,RERS BY OCctJI>ATIONAL DIVISIONS, GROUP
AND FAMILY OF THE NATIONAL CLASSIFICATION OF OCCUPATIONS
The source of the data for the Subsidiary Table
is cols. 1 to 7 of the Main Table 'B-V, Occupa~
tional Clastlification by Sex of Persons. at Work
other than 'Cultivation' printed in Part ~I-B of the
Himachal Pradesh 1961 Census Report.
The Subsidiary Table shows per 10,000 distribution of workers other,'than those engaged in
cultivation and agricultural labour among various
Occupational DivisioIl,S, Groups and Families\of
National Classifi,cation of Occupations (N.C.O.)
for the Union Territory and'each of its districts.
':For each of th~ cols. 2 to 7 of the Subsidiary
Table the 'Total' bf 'All Divisions' has been taken
as 10,000, first distributed into ten Occupationa.l
Divisions, i.e., 0 to ~ and X, and then fur;ther distribute9- into various Occupational Groups and
\ ,Families.
The fignres have been rounded off to 'integers.
Thus the total' of Occupational Groups under a
Division is equal to the, total figure given for the
Division. Similarly the total of the Families under
an Ocoupational Group is equ.al to the total figure
given for the Occupational Group.
I

In the Subsidiary Table

°N=NilNegligible
=

The rounding off is such that for each col. of
subsidiary table,
(i) The total of all the Divisions is equal to
10,000.
(ii) The total of all the Occupational Groups
in a J!ivision is equal to the figure of that
Division.
(iii) The total of all the Families in, all, Occupational Group is equal to the figure of
that Occupational Group. But it is not
so in many cas~, because certain Families which have figures less than 1 per
cent of the respective Divisions (0·5 %
in case of Division 7-8) have beenpmitted
frqm the Main Table.
The district of Kfunaur is entirely rural and
hence nil urban information for this district.
I

The National Classification of Occupations
(N.C.O.) Divisions, Groups and Families are indio
cated by their code numbers in the Subsidiary
Table. For study of these concepts the code
structure 9f the National Classification of Occu.
pations may be seen in the Introduction'portion
of this publication, which is printed there as
'Appendix III' tQ Introduction.

SUBSIDIARY TABLE B-XIV PART A·l
DISTRIBUTION OF 1,000 SAMPLE HOUSEHOTJDS ENGAGED IN HOUSEHOLD INDUSTRY
ONLY BY PRINCIPAL HOUSEHOLD INDUSTRY AND WORKING STRENGTH

IN AL~ AREAS
The source for this Subsidiary Table (S.T.) is
Main Table (M.T.) 'B-XIV- -Sample Households
engaged only in Household Industry classified by
Principal Household Industry in All Areas; Part
A-Households classified by Major Groups of

Principal Household Industry and Number of
Persons engaged', appearing in Part III of the
Himachal Pradesh 1961 Census Report.i,The method
adopted for:working out the figures'isldetailed
below:-

Col. 9 (Tota.l) S.T.

x 1,000

CoL 10 (Tohl) S.T.

other industries, for rural and urban areas separately. The figures have been rounded off to two

SUBSIDIARY TABLE B-XIV PART B·1
DISTRIBUTION OF 1,000 SAMPLE HOUSEHOLDS ENGAGED IN HOUSEHOLD INDUSTRY
ONLY BY PRINCIPAL HOUSEHOLD INDUSTRY (DIVISION/MAJOR/MINOR
GROUPS IN ALL AREAS
The source of the data for this Subsidiary Table
(S.T.) is the Main Table (M.T.) 'B-XIV-8ample
Households engaged only in Household Industry
classified by Principal Household Industry in All
Areas-Part B-Households olassified by Minor
Groups of Principal Household Industry', printed
in Part III of the Himachal Pradesh 1961 Census
Report. The following method has been adopted
for working out the :figures.
In Part B of the Main Table, the sample households, who are engaged in household industry
only, have been classified by code numbers of
I.S.1.0., minor groups of principal household
Division:

Col. 3 (Total) S.T.

industries indicating side by side the standard
descriptions of the minor groups. In the Subsidiary
Table the distribution of 1,000 sample households,
engaged in household industry only, has' been
shown by principal household industry, classified
by code numbers of 1.8.1.C., divisions, major groups
and minor groups of principal household industries
indicating side by side their standard descriptions. From the Main Table, the figures of minor
groups have been first grouped into major groups
and then intb divisions, and the figures, thereafter
worked out for the Subsidiary Table as indicated
below:=1,000

Col. 4 (Division-Rural) M.T.
Col. 4 (Rural) S.T.
Col. 3 (Division-Total) M.T.

x 1,000

Col. 5 (Diviaion-Urban) M.T.
X 1,000

Col. 5 (Urban) S.T. Col. 3 (Division-Total) M.T.
Col. 3 (Major Group-Total) M.T.
Major Group:

Col. 3 (Total) S.T.
Col. 3 (Division-Total) M.T.

X 1,000

-'

XI,ODO

Col. 4 (Rural) S.T.
Col. 3 (Division-Total) M.T.
Col. 5 (Major Group-Urban) M.T.
Col. 5 (Urban) S.T.
Col. 3 (Division-Total) M.T.

x 1,000

and. so on.
the total of minor group comes to the figure for
major group in which they fall.

The figures have been rounded off to two places
of decimals. The figures have been rounded off in
such a way that the total of major groups is equal
to the figures given for their division. Similarly

The district of Kinnaur is entirely rural and hence

nil urban information for this district.
